1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	
5	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
6	\label{sec:stat_framework}
7	
8	The primary family comprises six tests (three target judges $\times$ two axes), corrected via Holm-Bonferroni at $\alpha = 0.05$; Gemini-2.5-Flash serves as confirmatory replication and is excluded from family-wise error rate (FWER) control; as a sensitivity check, all eight tests remain significant under the expanded family (Appendix~\ref{app:holm8}).
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.
10	Analyses outside this grid are classified as either \emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \S\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \S\ref{sec:consistency}).
11	Exploratory analyses use uncorrected $p$-values; confirmatory analyses (pipeline validity) are evaluated at $\alpha = 0.05$ without additional correction.
12	Bradley-Terry parameters \citep{bradley1952rank,luce1959individual} are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \citep{hunter2004mm}, using the \texttt{choix} Python library's default L2 regularization ($\alpha = 10^{-6}$).
13	In binary-choice BT models, independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA) is structurally satisfied; the direction asymmetry in Appendix~\ref{app:bt_gof} (formality: formal-origin 88.9\% vs.\ casual-origin 52.6\%, Fisher $p = 0.015$) reflects heterogeneous treatment effects rather than IIA violation.
14	All bootstrap confidence intervals are percentile CIs from 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42; we verify bootstrap stability by confirming that halving the resample count changes interval widths by $<$1pp.
15	GEE models use exchangeable working correlation with sandwich standard errors (robust to correlation misspecification), justified by near-zero estimated within-pair correlations ($\hat{\alpha} \in [-0.25, +0.11]$; Appendix~\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).
16	A prospective power analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}) confirms that our sample sizes ($n = 86$--$88$ pairs) provide $>$90\% power to detect BT effects of the observed magnitude ($P \geq 0.63$), whereas Likert requires $4$--$206\times$ the sample size for equivalent power (Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Model selection.}
19	Target judges span four architectures from independent providers: GPT-4o (\texttt{2024-08-06}; OpenAI), Qwen3-32B-Instruct (Alibaba; also serving as rewriter), Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (Meta), and Gemini-2.5-Flash (Google).
20	This diversity guards against provider-specific artifacts in both training data and RLHF reward modeling; Claude Sonnet~4 (Anthropic) provides discriminative validity evidence (\S\ref{sec:cross_judge}), serving as a fifth judge from a provider not represented in the primary family.
21	All judges are accessed via their respective APIs with temperature 0 (deterministic decoding) and default system prompts to ensure reproducibility.

65	\section{Analysis}
66	\label{sec:analysis}
67	
68	
69	\subsection{The Likert Blindspot}
70	\label{sec:likert_pairwise}
71	
72	\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}
73	Applying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = {-}0.04$, $p = 0.91$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).
74	This is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments, yet one detects bias and the other does not.
75	A $4\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\%$ ($p < 0.0001$). If the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.
76	Bootstrap subsampling confirms $d$ remains near zero as $n$ grows (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).
77	Post hoc, attenuation follows a severity spectrum: complete at moderate bias ($d \to 0$, BT 63--67\%) and partial at stronger effects (${>}60\%$ loss, BT ${>}70\%$), explaining four cross-benchmark exceptions ($\ddagger$, Table~\ref{tab:cross_bench}).
78	
79	RATE and CSD are complementary (Likert ATE vs.\ forced-choice BT); the Blindspot arises from Likert tie rates of 58--73\%, unresolved by 100-point continuous scaling (all three judges $p > 0.15$; Appendix~\ref{app:likert100}).
80	
81	
82	\paragraph{Sensitivity hierarchy across measurement methods.}
83	Seven measurement methods applied to the same formality data confirm the Blindspot is not test-specific: both pairwise methods detect the formality preference while all five Likert-based methods fail regardless of scaling assumption (interval, ordinal, or nonparametric), with verbosity showing the same pattern (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}, Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}; verbosity replication in Appendix~\ref{app:verb_sensitivity}).
84	
85	Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\ \citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): across six cells, Likert requires $4$--$206\times$ the current $n$ ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}) to detect effects BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \leq 100$.
86	Under format equivalence, observing 8/8 primary cells as Blindspot has binomial probability $p = 0.004$; non-tie concordance (76.6\%, $p < .001$; \S\ref{sec:likert_pairwise}) provides a complementary formal test of format divergence.
87	\paragraph{Interpreting the divergence.}
88	The measurement-mode divergence admits three non-exclusive interpretations: (a)~\emph{Likert attenuation}, where tie inflation and residual signal loss reduce Likert sensitivity below detection thresholds; (b)~\emph{pairwise amplification}, where forced-choice comparison inflates micro-preferences into apparent biases \citep{jeong2025comparative}; (c)~\emph{construct divergence}, where the two formats measure different constructs (absolute quality vs.\ ordinal preference; \citealt{tripathi2025pairwise}).
89	Six experimental controls (\S\ref{sec:amplification}) bound the amplification factor but cannot eliminate it; the attenuation evidence ($d \to 0$ at $n = 355$) is necessary but not sufficient for causal identification.
90	Non-tie concordance analysis partially adjudicates: among 201 Likert non-tie pairs (pooled across judges and axes), 76.6\% agree with pairwise direction [70.3\%, 81.9\%], $p < 0.001$; point-biserial correlations are uniformly positive ($r = 0.20$--$0.45$, 7/9 significant; Appendix~\ref{app:concordance}), evidence more consistent with measurement attenuation than construct divergence, though partial construct divergence remains at weaker effect sizes.
91	Claude's benchmark-dependent profile illustrates why aggregate BT is insufficient for bias attribution: LLMBar formality shows near-null sensitivity (Style OR n.s.) while AEval verbosity reveals significant sensitivity (Style OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$), a pattern disambiguated only by per-axis GEE decomposition (\S\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\ref{app:claude_tost}).
92	All three interpretations converge on one practical conclusion: single-format audits risk incomplete bias coverage; the Blindspot manifests in 10/14 bias-positive cells, with four exceptions ($\ddagger$) confirming effect-size dependence.
93	For alignment pipelines where pairwise judgments serve as reward signals, this implies that biases embedded during RLHF training may go undetected by Likert-based evaluation at standard benchmark scales.
94

98	\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\% power crossings: sign test $n \approx 62$, BT $n \approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}
99	\label{fig:power_curve}
100	\end{figure}
101	
102	\subsection{Per-Axis Confound Structure}
103	\label{sec:direction}
104	
105	GEE conditional decomposition \citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).
106	After controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant (GPT-4o: OR $= 2.58$, $p = 0.002$; Qwen3-32B: OR $= 2.15$, $p < 0.0001$, $n = 355$; Llama-70B: OR $= 4.81$, $p < 0.0001$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}); the significant originality ORs confirm residual confounding.
107	For formality, both style preference and quality correlation contribute (a conditional effect); the two-predictor GEE provides a parsimonious decomposition rather than causal identification; omitted variables (text length, lexical complexity) may partly confound both terms.
108	This per-axis heterogeneity has direct implications for bias mitigation: verbosity bias can be addressed by style-level interventions (e.g., length normalization or debiased prompting), whereas formality bias requires disentangling genuine quality correlates from surface-level preferences, a fundamentally harder problem that aggregate bias metrics cannot even detect.

Verbosity is style-dominated; formality shows varying confound structure.}

139	
140	The confound structure is consistent across all four target judges for verbosity (style-dominated at $n = 86$--$88$: Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; confirmed at $n = 330$: Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$); formality is conditionally style-driven in three judges but originality-dominated in Gemini; register is judge-dependent (Figure~\ref{fig:gee_decomp}).
141	Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (Appendix~\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.
142	The Gemini exception (formality Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.) demonstrates that the decomposition is not uniformly biased toward detecting style effects; judges with weaker style sensitivity produce correspondingly weaker Style ORs.
143	
144	\begin{figure}[t]
145	\centering
146	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/gee_decomposition.pdf}
147	\caption{GEE conditional decomposition across four judges ($n = 88$, LLMBar). Verbosity (left) is style-dominated: Style OR $\gg 1$, Originality OR $< 1$. Formality (right) shows joint contributions from both predictors. Dashed line: OR $= 1$ (no effect). Hatched bars: Originality OR. Log scale.}
148	\label{fig:gee_decomp}
149	\end{figure}
150	
151	\paragraph{Sample-size sensitivity.}
152	At $n = 355$, the Qwen3-32B formality Style OR drops from $4.20$ to $2.15$ ($-49\%$), consistent with small-sample upward bias \citep{nemes2009bias}; the regime classification remains stable (both effects significant; Orig OR $4.70 \to 4.99$).
153	Verbosity $n = 330$ confirms 51--72\% GEE shrinkage (Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:verb_n355}), preserving the style-dominated classification; Likert begins detecting the strongest effects at this $n$, reframing the Blindspot as a $3$--$4\times$ efficiency gap rather than absolute failure.
154	This convergence at larger $n$ is practically important: it means that labs with sufficient evaluation budgets ($n > 300$) can detect the strongest biases with Likert alone, but typical benchmark sizes ($n = 50$--$200$) systematically miss them.
155	
156	\subsection{Cross-Judge and Cross-Axis Replication}
157	\label{sec:cross_judge}
158	
159	Two independent judges replicate the formality Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.461$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.534$).
160	Gemini-2.5-Flash shows a weaker, originality-dominated formality pattern (confirmatory: BT 57.4\%, $p = 0.031$; Likert $d = {-}0.08$, n.s.; Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.; Orig OR $= 2.51$, $p = 0.002$; \S\ref{sec:direction}).
161	Claude Sonnet~4 shows near-null sensitivity on LLMBar for both axes (BT $\leq 0.562$, n.s.), providing discriminative validity: CSD does not uniformly inflate bias estimates across all judges.
162	
163	Verbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all four target judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\%, Llama-70B 73.5\%, Gemini 70.8\%; all $p \leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all ($p = 0.284$--$0.403$).
164	GEE confirms style dominance (Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$; Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
165	Both rewriting directions favor verbose responses (90.7\%/80.6\%); length-controlled Style OR is unchanged ($<$2\%), and an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds evaluator-producer overlap to a 22\% reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
166	Length-controlled regression confirms 60\% of the Flesch-Kincaid (FK) cross-axis effect is mediated by sentence length (Sobel $z = 2.15$, $p = .031$); under the assumed causal structure (Figure~\ref{fig:dag}), FK acts as a plausible mediator ($S \to \text{FK} \to Y$; \citealp{pearl2009causality}), yielding a direct-effect Style OR of $2.15$ for GPT-4o (total OR $= 2.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:fk_length}).
167	
168	These per-axis patterns selectively replicate across benchmarks (6/24 possible cells; Figure~\ref{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}), targeting the strongest effects: Llama-70B formality on AlpacaEval (BT 75.0\%, Style OR $= 7.64$) is strong enough for Likert detection ($\ddagger$ rows), confirming the severity spectrum.
169	The selective replication strategy prioritizes cells where effects are strongest, providing an informative lower bound on cross-benchmark generalization without requiring exhaustive evaluation of all judge--axis--benchmark combinations.
170	
171	\begin{figure}[t]
172	\centering
173	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/cross_benchmark_heatmap.pdf}
174	\caption{Pairwise BT win probabilities across five judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes. Bold: $p < .05$; color intensity encodes significance level. Grey $\times$: not tested. Claude cross-benchmark effects are quality-driven (\S\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\ref{app:claude_crossbench}). Full results in Table~\ref{tab:cross_bench}.}
175	\label{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}
176	\end{figure}
177	
178	\subsection{Robustness}
179	\label{sec:nli_validation}
180	
181	\paragraph{Content preservation.}
182	Two independent annotators evaluated 100 NLI-scored pairs (50 per axis; 70 gate-passed, 30 gate-failed), confirming $98.6\%$ gate pass precision ($69/70$; Gwet's AC$_1 = 0.96$; Appendix~\ref{app:annotation_agreement}).
183	\label{sec:nli_threshold}
184	BT remains stable (${\leq}3$pp variation) across 11 NLI thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:nli_sweep}), confirming that results are not artifacts of a particular gating stringency.
185	Annotators judged only 49--66\% of NLI-passed pairs as showing detectable style change, suggesting observed effects represent a diluted lower bound on true style sensitivity: the NLI gate retains pairs with subtle rewrites that human annotators cannot detect, yet judges still systematically prefer one variant.
186	
187	\paragraph{Detection vs.\ amplification.}
188	\label{sec:amplification}
189	\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).
190	The divergence cannot be definitively resolved without human ground-truth measurements; six controls bound amplification risk: (i)~NLI ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs (BT 41.7\%); (ii)~Claude shows benchmark-dependent sensitivity (formality Style OR n.s.; AEval verbosity OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation inconsistent with uniform amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE; (v)~debiased prompting ($-5.2$/$-13.1$pp), bounding the amplification factor at ${\sim}9$--$17\%$ of the total BT effect; (vi)~four-architecture replication with consistent GEE structure.
191	Register is exploratory with evaluator-producer overlap and judge-dependent effects (Table~\ref{tab:gee}).
192	Same-style double-rewrite controls confirm that any residual rewriting artifact is absorbed by the GEE originality term rather than inflating the style coefficient.
193	The combination of NLI validation and amplification controls establishes that while the exact magnitude of style sensitivity remains bounded rather than point-identified, the qualitative finding (systematic style preferences invisible to Likert) is robust across judges, axes, and measurement assumptions.
194	
```

verbosity replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:verb_sensitivity}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\\ \\citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): across six cells, Likert requires $4$--$206\\times$ the current $n$ ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}) to detect effects BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \\leq 100$.\nUnder format equivalence, observing 8/8 primary cells as Blindspot has binomial probability $p = 0.004$; non-tie concordance (76.6\\%, $p < .001$; \\S\\ref{sec:likert_pairwise}) provides a complementary formal test of format divergence.\n\\paragraph{Interpreting the divergence.}\nThe measurement-mode divergence admits three non-exclusive interpretations: (a)~\\emph{Likert attenuation}, where tie inflation and residual signal loss reduce Likert sensitivity below detection thresholds; (b)~\\emph{pairwise amplification}, where forced-choice comparison inflates micro-preferences into apparent biases \\citep{jeong2025comparative}; (c)~\\emph{construct divergence}, where the two formats measure different constructs (absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference; 70 gate-passed, 30 gate-failed), confirming $98.6\\%$ gate pass precision ($69/70$; Gwet's AC$_1 = 0.96$; Appendix~\\ref{app:annotation_agreement}).\n\\label{sec:nli_threshold}\nBT remains stable (${\\leq}3$pp variation) across 11 NLI thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_sweep}), confirming that results are not artifacts of a particular gating stringency.\nAnnotators judged only 49--66\\% of NLI-passed pairs as showing detectable style change, suggesting observed effects represent a diluted lower bound on true style sensitivity: the NLI gate retains pairs with subtle rewrites that human annotators cannot detect, yet judges still systematically prefer one variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection vs.\\ amplification.}\n\\label{sec:amplification}\n\\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).\nThe divergence cannot be definitively resolved without human ground-truth measurements; six controls bound amplification risk: (i)~NLI ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs (BT 41.7\\%); (ii)~Claude shows benchmark-dependent sensitivity (formality Style OR n.s.; AEval verbosity OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation inconsistent with uniform amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE; (v)~debiased prompting ($-5.2$/$-13.1$pp), bounding the amplification factor at ${\\sim}9$--$17\\%$ of the total BT effect; (vi)~four-architecture replication with consistent GEE structure.\nRegister is exploratory with evaluator-producer overlap and judge-dependent effects (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm that any residual rewriting artifact is absorbed by the GEE originality term rather than inflating the style coefficient.\nThe combination of NLI validation and amplification controls establishes that while the exact magnitude of style sensitivity remains bounded rather than point-identified, the qualitative finding (systematic style preferences invisible to Likert) is robust across judges, axes, and measurement assumptions.\n",
      "numLines": 194,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 194
    }
  }
}

38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\label{sec:limitations}
40	
41	\textbf{Residual confounding.}
42	The NLI gate blocks content changes but cannot block quality perception shifts mediated by style itself.
43	Although formality effects survive logistic adjustment (OR $= 2.32$, $p = 0.006$), residual confounding is not fully eliminated; definitive resolution requires human ground-truth comparison of Likert and pairwise judgments on identical pairs.
44	
45	\textbf{Detection vs.\ amplification.}
46	The Blindspot may partly reflect pairwise format amplification \citep{jeong2025comparative} rather than pure detection.
47	Six controls bound this risk (\S\ref{sec:amplification}), but we cannot precisely quantify the amplification factor without a ground-truth bias magnitude.
48	Qwen3-32B serves as both rewriter and judge; an independent GPT-4o rewriter yields 22\% lower Style OR, confirming the Blindspot persists but effect-size precision varies with rewriter choice (Appendix~\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).
49	
50	\textbf{Scope.}
51	CSD applies only to surface-level axes whose rewrites preserve semantic content; a tone pilot (assertive $\to$ hedging) was rejected by the NLI gate.
52	Primary analyses use LLMBar ($n = 100$) with five judges; generalization to additional axes, benchmarks, and judges remains open.
53	Style sensitivity may vary across model versions (\S\ref{sec:nli_validation}), and GEE odds-ratio estimates require large-sample validation to converge from initial upper-bound estimates ($49$--$72\%$ shrinkage at $n = 330$--$355$).
54	
55	\bibliography{references}
56	
57	\input{sections/appendix}
58	
59	\end{document}
60

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nThe primary family comprises six tests (three target judges $\\times$ two axes), corrected via Holm-Bonferroni at $\\alpha = 0.05$; Gemini-2.5-Flash serves as confirmatory replication and is excluded from family-wise error rate (FWER) control; as a sensitivity check, all eight tests remain significant under the expanded family (Appendix~\\ref{app:holm8}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.\nAnalyses outside this grid are classified as either \\emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \\S\\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \\emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \\S\\ref{sec:consistency}).\nExploratory analyses use uncorrected $p$-values; confirmatory analyses (pipeline validity) are evaluated at $\\alpha = 0.05$ without additional correction.\nBradley-Terry parameters \\citep{bradley1952rank,luce1959individual} are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \\citep{hunter2004mm}, using the \\texttt{choix} Python library's default L2 regularization ($\\alpha = 10^{-6}$).\nIn binary-choice BT models, independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA) is structurally satisfied; the direction asymmetry in Appendix~\\ref{app:bt_gof} (formality: formal-origin 88.9\\% vs.\\ casual-origin 52.6\\%, Fisher $p = 0.015$) reflects heterogeneous treatment effects rather than IIA violation.\nAll bootstrap confidence intervals are percentile CIs from 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42; we verify bootstrap stability by confirming that halving the resample count changes interval widths by $<$1pp.\nGEE models use exchangeable working correlation with sandwich standard errors (robust to correlation misspecification), justified by near-zero estimated within-pair correlations ($\\hat{\\alpha} \\in [-0.25, +0.11]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).\nA prospective power analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}) confirms that our sample sizes ($n = 86$--$88$ pairs) provide $>$90\\% power to detect BT effects of the observed magnitude ($P \\geq 0.63$), whereas Likert requires $4$--$206\\times$ the sample size for equivalent power (Figure~\\ref{fig:power_curve}).\n\n\\paragraph{Model selection.}\nTarget judges span four architectures from independent providers: GPT-4o (\\texttt{2024-08-06}; OpenAI), Qwen3-32B-Instruct (Alibaba; also serving as rewriter), Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (Meta), and Gemini-2.5-Flash (Google).\nThis diversity guards against provider-specific artifacts in both training data and RLHF reward modeling; Claude Sonnet~4 (Anthropic) provides discriminative validity evidence (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}), serving as a fifth judge from a provider not represented in the primary family.\nAll judges are accessed via their respective APIs with temperature 0 (deterministic decoding) and default system prompts to ensure reproducibility.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Primary results on LLMBar ($n = 88$ formality, $86$ verbosity, except where noted) across five judges and three style axes. BT $> 0.50$ indicates style preference; $\\dagger$ marks Blindspot cases (BT significant, Likert not). Cross-benchmark replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench}. Negative $d$: styled variants scored lower.}\\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize $^b$ Scaled replication ($n = 355$). Qwen3 serves as both rewriter and judge; independent GPT-4o rewriter yields 22\\% lower Style OR (App.~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).}\\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize Form.\\ direction asymmetry: formal-origin 78.8\\% vs.\\ casual-origin 54.5\\%; GEE-adj.\\ Style OR$=$2.58 ($p{=}.002$; App.~\\ref{app:direction}).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Bradley-Terry win probabilities across four target judges and three style axes. Filled diamonds: significant ($p < 0.05$); open diamonds: non-significant. Error bars: 95\\% bootstrap CI. The dashed line at 0.50 indicates no style preference. Verbosity shows the strongest and most consistent bias across all four judges.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\n\\subsection{The Likert Blindspot}\n\\label{sec:likert_pairwise}\n\n\\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}\nApplying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \\citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = {-}0.04$, $p = 0.91$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).\nThis is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments, yet one detects bias and the other does not.\nA $4\\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\\%$ ($p < 0.0001$). If the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.\nBootstrap subsampling confirms $d$ remains near zero as $n$ grows (Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).\nPost hoc, attenuation follows a severity spectrum: complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$, BT 63--67\\%) and partial at stronger effects (${>}60\\%$ loss, BT ${>}70\\%$), explaining four cross-benchmark exceptions ($\\ddagger$, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench}).\n\nRATE and CSD are complementary (Likert ATE vs.\\ forced-choice BT); the Blindspot arises from Likert tie rates of 58--73\\%, unresolved by 100-point continuous scaling (all three judges $p > 0.15$; Appendix~\\ref{app:likert100}).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity hierarchy across measurement methods.}\nSeven measurement methods applied to the same formality data confirm the Blindspot is not test-specific: both pairwise methods detect the formality preference while all five Likert-based methods fail regardless of scaling assumption (interval, ordinal, or nonparametric), with verbosity showing the same pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity}, Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}; verbosity replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:verb_sensitivity}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\\ \\citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): across six cells, Likert requires $4$--$206\\times$ the current $n$ ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}) to detect effects BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \\leq 100$.\nUnder format equivalence, observing 8/8 primary cells as Blindspot has binomial probability $p = 0.004$; non-tie concordance (76.6\\%, $p < .001$; \\S\\ref{sec:likert_pairwise}) provides a complementary formal test of format divergence.\n\\paragraph{Interpreting the divergence.}\nThe measurement-mode divergence admits three non-exclusive interpretations: (a)~\\emph{Likert attenuation}, where tie inflation and residual signal loss reduce Likert sensitivity below detection thresholds; (b)~\\emph{pairwise amplification}, where forced-choice comparison inflates micro-preferences into apparent biases \\citep{jeong2025comparative}; (c)~\\emph{construct divergence}, where the two formats measure different constructs (absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference; \\citealt{tripathi2025pairwise}).\nSix experimental controls (\\S\\ref{sec:amplification}) bound the amplification factor but cannot eliminate it; the attenuation evidence ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$) is necessary but not sufficient for causal identification.\nNon-tie concordance analysis partially adjudicates: among 201 Likert non-tie pairs (pooled across judges and axes), 76.6\\% agree with pairwise direction [70.3\\%, 81.9\\%], $p < 0.001$; point-biserial correlations are uniformly positive ($r = 0.20$--$0.45$, 7/9 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:concordance}), evidence more consistent with measurement attenuation than construct divergence, though partial construct divergence remains at weaker effect sizes.\nClaude's benchmark-dependent profile illustrates why aggregate BT is insufficient for bias attribution: LLMBar formality shows near-null sensitivity (Style OR n.s.) while AEval verbosity reveals significant sensitivity (Style OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$), a pattern disambiguated only by per-axis GEE decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_tost}).\nAll three interpretations converge on one practical conclusion: single-format audits risk incomplete bias coverage; the Blindspot manifests in 10/14 bias-positive cells, with four exceptions ($\\ddagger$) confirming effect-size dependence.\nFor alignment pipelines where pairwise judgments serve as reward signals, this implies that biases embedded during RLHF training may go undetected by Likert-based evaluation at standard benchmark scales.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/power_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\\% power crossings: sign test $n \\approx 62$, BT $n \\approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \\approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \\approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}\n\\label{fig:power_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Axis Confound Structure}\n\\label{sec:direction}\n\nGEE conditional decomposition \\citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).\nAfter controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant (GPT-4o: OR $= 2.58$, $p = 0.002$; Qwen3-32B: OR $= 2.15$, $p < 0.0001$, $n = 355$; Llama-70B: OR $= 4.81$, $p < 0.0001$; Table~\\ref{tab:gee}); the significant originality ORs confirm residual confounding.\nFor formality, both style preference and quality correlation contribute (a conditional effect); the two-predictor GEE provides a parsimonious decomposition rather than causal identification; omitted variables (text length, lexical complexity) may partly confound both terms.\nThis per-axis heterogeneity has direct implications for bias mitigation: verbosity bias can be addressed by style-level interventions (e.g., length normalization or debiased prompting), whereas formality bias requires disentangling genuine quality correlates from surface-level preferences, a fundamentally harder problem that aggregate bias metrics cannot even detect.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{GEE conditional decomposition (exchangeable correlation). Style OR: style-direction odds ratio; Orig OR: original-preference odds ratio (OR $> 1$ favors original). $n{=}88$ ORs are upward-biased estimates; scaled replications ($n = 330$--$355$) show $49$--$72\\%$ shrinkage \\citep{nemes2009bias}. Verbosity is style-dominated; formality shows varying confound structure.}\n\\label{tab:gee}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llclc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Style OR} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Originality OR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nConfig & OR & $p$ & OR & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 verb.\\ & 13.10 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.49 & .096 \\\\\nGPT-4o verb.\\ & 11.34 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.45 & .062 \\\\\nLlama-70B verb.\\ & 8.21 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.38 & .020 \\\\\nGemini verb.\\ & 8.98 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.46 & .061 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o form.\\ & 2.58 & .002 & 2.32 & .006 \\\\\nQwen3 form.\\ & 4.20 & .0002 & 4.70 & .0001 \\\\\nQwen3 form.$^a$ & 2.15 & ${<}.0001$ & 4.99 & ${<}.0001$ \\\\\nLlama-70B form.\\ & 4.81 & ${<}.0001$ & 3.12 & .0007 \\\\\nGemini form.\\ & 1.64 & .101 & 2.51 & .002 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o reg.\\ & 1.88 & .029 & 2.21 & .006 \\\\\nQwen3 reg.\\ & 1.91 & .046 & 5.72 & ${<}.0001$ \\\\\nLlama-70B reg.\\ & 3.33 & .0003 & 3.07 & .0008 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\footnotesize $^a$ $n = 355$; form.\\ $n{=}88$, verb.\\ $n{=}86$. 95\\% CIs in Appendix.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe confound structure is consistent across all four target judges for verbosity (style-dominated at $n = 86$--$88$: Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; confirmed at $n = 330$: Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$); formality is conditionally style-driven in three judges but originality-dominated in Gemini; register is judge-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:gee_decomp}).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.\nThe Gemini exception (formality Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.) demonstrates that the decomposition is not uniformly biased toward detecting style effects; judges with weaker style sensitivity produce correspondingly weaker Style ORs.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/gee_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{GEE conditional decomposition across four judges ($n = 88$, LLMBar). Verbosity (left) is style-dominated: Style OR $\\gg 1$, Originality OR $< 1$. Formality (right) shows joint contributions from both predictors. Dashed line: OR $= 1$ (no effect). Hatched bars: Originality OR. Log scale.}\n\\label{fig:gee_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Sample-size sensitivity.}\nAt $n = 355$, the Qwen3-32B formality Style OR drops from $4.20$ to $2.15$ ($-49\\%$), consistent with small-sample upward bias \\citep{nemes2009bias}; the regime classification remains stable (both effects significant; Orig OR $4.70 \\to 4.99$).\nVerbosity $n = 330$ confirms 51--72\\% GEE shrinkage (Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:verb_n355}), preserving the style-dominated classification; Likert begins detecting the strongest effects at this $n$, reframing the Blindspot as a $3$--$4\\times$ efficiency gap rather than absolute failure.\nThis convergence at larger $n$ is practically important: it means that labs with sufficient evaluation budgets ($n > 300$) can detect the strongest biases with Likert alone, but typical benchmark sizes ($n = 50$--$200$) systematically miss them.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Judge and Cross-Axis Replication}\n\\label{sec:cross_judge}\n\nTwo independent judges replicate the formality Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.461$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.534$).\nGemini-2.5-Flash shows a weaker, originality-dominated formality pattern (confirmatory: BT 57.4\\%, $p = 0.031$; Likert $d = {-}0.08$, n.s.; Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.; Orig OR $= 2.51$, $p = 0.002$; \\S\\ref{sec:direction}).\nClaude Sonnet~4 shows near-null sensitivity on LLMBar for both axes (BT $\\leq 0.562$, n.s.), providing discriminative validity: CSD does not uniformly inflate bias estimates across all judges.\n\nVerbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all four target judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\\%, Llama-70B 73.5\\%, Gemini 70.8\\%; all $p \\leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all ($p = 0.284$--$0.403$).\nGEE confirms style dominance (Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$; Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\nBoth rewriting directions favor verbose responses (90.7\\%/80.6\\%); length-controlled Style OR is unchanged ($<$2\\%), and an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds evaluator-producer overlap to a 22\\% reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).\nLength-controlled regression confirms 60\\% of the Flesch-Kincaid (FK) cross-axis effect is mediated by sentence length (Sobel $z = 2.15$, $p = .031$); under the assumed causal structure (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag}), FK acts as a plausible mediator ($S \\to \\text{FK} \\to Y$; \\citealp{pearl2009causality}), yielding a direct-effect Style OR of $2.15$ for GPT-4o (total OR $= 2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:fk_length}).\n\nThese per-axis patterns selectively replicate across benchmarks (6/24 possible cells; Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}), targeting the strongest effects: Llama-70B formality on AlpacaEval (BT 75.0\\%, Style OR $= 7.64$) is strong enough for Likert detection ($\\ddagger$ rows), confirming the severity spectrum.\nThe selective replication strategy prioritizes cells where effects are strongest, providing an informative lower bound on cross-benchmark generalization without requiring exhaustive evaluation of all judge--axis--benchmark combinations.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/cross_benchmark_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Pairwise BT win probabilities across five judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes. Bold: $p < .05$; color intensity encodes significance level. Grey $\\times$: not tested. Claude cross-benchmark effects are quality-driven (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_crossbench}). Full results in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench}.}\n\\label{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nli_validation}\n\n\\paragraph{Content preservation.}\nTwo independent annotators evaluated 100 NLI-scored pairs (50 per axis; 70 gate-passed, 30 gate-failed), confirming $98.6\\%$ gate pass precision ($69/70$; Gwet's AC$_1 = 0.96$; Appendix~\\ref{app:annotation_agreement}).\n\\label{sec:nli_threshold}\nBT remains stable (${\\leq}3$pp variation) across 11 NLI thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_sweep}), confirming that results are not artifacts of a particular gating stringency.\nAnnotators judged only 49--66\\% of NLI-passed pairs as showing detectable style change, suggesting observed effects represent a diluted lower bound on true style sensitivity: the NLI gate retains pairs with subtle rewrites that human annotators cannot detect, yet judges still systematically prefer one variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection vs.\\ amplification.}\n\\label{sec:amplification}\n\\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).\nThe divergence cannot be definitively resolved without human ground-truth measurements; six controls bound amplification risk: (i)~NLI ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs (BT 41.7\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude shows benchmark-dependent sensitivity (formality Style OR n.s.; AEval verbosity OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation inconsistent with uniform amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE; (v)~debiased prompting ($-5.2$/$-13.1$pp), bounding the amplification factor at ${\\sim}9$--$17\\%$ of the total BT effect; (vi)~four-architecture replication with consistent GEE structure.\nRegister is exploratory with evaluator-producer overlap and judge-dependent effects (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm that any residual rewriting artifact is absorbed by the GEE originality term rather than inflating the style coefficient.\nThe combination of NLI validation and amplification controls establishes that while the exact magnitude of style sensitivity remains bounded rather than point-identified, the qualitative finding (systematic style preferences invisible to Likert) is robust across judges, axes, and measurement assumptions.\n",

Gemini-2.5-Flash serves as confirmatory replication and is excluded from family-wise error rate (FWER) control; as a sensitivity check, all eight tests remain significant under the expanded family (Appendix~\\ref{app:holm8}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports both raw and adjusted $p$-values.\nAnalyses outside this grid are classified as either \\emph{exploratory} (NLI threshold sensitivity sweep, \\S\\ref{sec:nli_threshold}) or \\emph{confirmatory} (consistency filter validation, \\S\\ref{sec:consistency}).\nExploratory analyses use uncorrected $p$-values; confirmatory analyses (pipeline validity) are evaluated at $\\alpha = 0.05$ without additional correction.\nBradley-Terry parameters \\citep{bradley1952rank,luce1959individual} are estimated via spectral initialization followed by minorization-maximization \\citep{hunter2004mm}, using the \\texttt{choix} Python library's default L2 regularization ($\\alpha = 10^{-6}$).\nIn binary-choice BT models, independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA) is structurally satisfied; the direction asymmetry in Appendix~\\ref{app:bt_gof} (formality: formal-origin 88.9\\% vs.\\ casual-origin 52.6\\%, Fisher $p = 0.015$) reflects heterogeneous treatment effects rather than IIA violation.\nAll bootstrap confidence intervals are percentile CIs from 10{,}000 pair-level resamples with seed 42; we verify bootstrap stability by confirming that halving the resample count changes interval widths by $<$1pp.\nGEE models use exchangeable working correlation with sandwich standard errors (robust to correlation misspecification), justified by near-zero estimated within-pair correlations ($\\hat{\\alpha} \\in [-0.25, +0.11]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).\nA prospective power analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}) confirms that our sample sizes ($n = 86$--$88$ pairs) provide $>$90\\% power to detect BT effects of the observed magnitude ($P \\geq 0.63$), whereas Likert requires $4$--$206\\times$ the sample size for equivalent power (Figure~\\ref{fig:power_curve}).\n\n\\paragraph{Model selection.}\nTarget judges span four architectures from independent providers: GPT-4o (\\texttt{2024-08-06}; OpenAI), Qwen3-32B-Instruct (Alibaba; also serving as rewriter), Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (Meta), and Gemini-2.5-Flash (Google).\nThis diversity guards against provider-specific artifacts in both training data and RLHF reward modeling; Claude Sonnet~4 (Anthropic) provides discriminative validity evidence (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}), serving as a fifth judge from a provider not represented in the primary family.\nAll judges are accessed via their respective APIs with temperature 0 (deterministic decoding) and default system prompts to ensure reproducibility.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Primary results on LLMBar ($n = 88$ formality, $86$ verbosity, except where noted) across five judges and three style axes. BT $> 0.50$ indicates style preference; $\\dagger$ marks Blindspot cases (BT significant, Likert not). Cross-benchmark replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench}. BT: Bradley-Terry win probability (95\\% CIs in Appendix~\\ref{app:bt_ci}).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nJudge & Axis & BT $P$ & Likert $d$ ($p$) & Cat.\\ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o & Form.\\ & .630$^{***}$ & $-$.041 (.905) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & Verb.\\ & .760$^{***}$ & .049 (.403) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & Reg.\\ & .570$^{\\dagger}$ & .000 (.785) & $\\dagger_{\\text{e}}$ \\\\\n\\cmidrule{1-5}\nQwen3 & Form.\\ & .665$^{***}$ & $-$.052 (.461) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nQwen3 & Form.$^b$ & .675$^{***}$ & $-$.025 (.600) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nQwen3 & Verb.\\ & .773$^{***}$ & .036 (.395) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nQwen3 & Reg.\\ & .553 & $-$.073 (.373) & $\\dagger_{\\text{e}}$ \\\\\n\\cmidrule{1-5}\nLlama-70B & Form.\\ & .695$^{***}$ & .010 (.534) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nLlama-70B & Verb.\\ & .735$^{***}$ & .020 (.284) & $\\dagger$ \\\\\nLlama-70B & Reg.\\ & .635$^{\\dagger}$ & .024 (.524) & $\\dagger_{\\text{e}}$ \\\\\n\\cmidrule{1-5}\nGemini & Form.\\ & .574$^{*}$ & $-$.082 (.309) & $\\dagger_{\\text{c}}$ \\\\\nGemini & Verb.\\ & .708$^{***}$ & .051 (.370) & $\\dagger_{\\text{c}}$ \\\\\n\\cmidrule{1-5}\nClaude & Form.\\ & .562 & $-$.021 (.721) & --- \\\\\nClaude & Verb.\\ & .541 & $-$.039 (.743) & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize $^{***}\\!p < 0.001$; $^{*}\\!p < 0.05$ (Holm-adj.\\ for primary; raw for $_{\\text{c}}$). $\\dagger$: Blindspot (BT sig., Likert n.s.); ---: neither sig.; $_{\\text{e}}$: exploratory; $_{\\text{c}}$: confirmatory. Negative $d$: styled variants scored lower.}\\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize $^b$ Scaled replication ($n = 355$). Qwen3 serves as both rewriter and judge; independent GPT-4o rewriter yields 22\\% lower Style OR (App.~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).}\\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize Form.\\ direction asymmetry: formal-origin 78.8\\% vs.\\ casual-origin 54.5\\%; GEE-adj.\\ Style OR$=$2.58 ($p{=}.002$; App.~\\ref{app:direction}).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Bradley-Terry win probabilities across four target judges and three style axes. Filled diamonds: significant ($p < 0.05$); open diamonds: non-significant. Error bars: 95\\% bootstrap CI. The dashed line at 0.50 indicates no style preference. Verbosity shows the strongest and most consistent bias across all four judges.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\n\\subsection{The Likert Blindspot}\n\\label{sec:likert_pairwise}\n\n\\paragraph{Head-to-head comparison with RATE.}\nApplying RATE's Likert ATE methodology \\citep{reber2025rate} to our formality style-transformed pairs yields Cohen's $d = {-}0.04$, $p = 0.91$ (non-significant, $n = 88$); pairwise comparison on identical data reveals a 63.0\\% formal preference ($p = 0.0004$, Bradley-Terry on all 173 comparisons), confirmed by a consistency-filtered sign test (73.9\\%, $p = 0.006$, $n = 46$ consistent pairs).\nThis is the Likert Blindspot: the same data, the same style intervention, two measurement instruments, yet one detects bias and the other does not.\nA $4\\times$ scaled replication ($n = 355$) distinguishes measurement attenuation from insufficient power: the Likert effect size shrinks to $d = {-}0.025$ (near zero) while BT remains stable at $67.5\\%$ ($p < 0.0001$). If the gap were merely statistical power, $d$ would remain non-zero at larger $n$.\nBootstrap subsampling confirms $d$ remains near zero as $n$ grows (Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_attenuation}).\nPost hoc, attenuation follows a severity spectrum: complete at moderate bias ($d \\to 0$, BT 63--67\\%) and partial at stronger effects (${>}60\\%$ loss, BT ${>}70\\%$), explaining four cross-benchmark exceptions ($\\ddagger$, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench}).\n\nRATE and CSD are complementary (Likert ATE vs.\\ forced-choice BT); the Blindspot arises from Likert tie rates of 58--73\\%, unresolved by 100-point continuous scaling (all three judges $p > 0.15$; Appendix~\\ref{app:likert100}).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity hierarchy across measurement methods.}\nSeven measurement methods applied to the same formality data confirm the Blindspot is not test-specific: both pairwise methods detect the formality preference while all five Likert-based methods fail regardless of scaling assumption (interval, ordinal, or nonparametric), with verbosity showing the same pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity}, Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}; verbosity replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:verb_sensitivity}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:power_curve} plots detection probability at observed effect magnitudes (not post-hoc power; cf.\\ \\citealt{hoenig2001abuse}): across six cells, Likert requires $4$--$206\\times$ the current $n$ ($336$--$27{,}159$ pairs; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}) to detect effects BT identifies at $p < 0.001$ with $n \\leq 100$.\nUnder format equivalence, observing 8/8 primary cells as Blindspot has binomial probability $p = 0.004$; non-tie concordance (76.6\\%, $p < .001$; \\S\\ref{sec:likert_pairwise}) provides a complementary formal test of format divergence.\n\\paragraph{Interpreting the divergence.}\nThe measurement-mode divergence admits three non-exclusive interpretations: (a)~\\emph{Likert attenuation}, where tie inflation and residual signal loss reduce Likert sensitivity below detection thresholds; (b)~\\emph{pairwise amplification}, where forced-choice comparison inflates micro-preferences into apparent biases \\citep{jeong2025comparative}; (c)~\\emph{construct divergence}, where the two formats measure different constructs (absolute quality vs.\\ ordinal preference; \\citealt{tripathi2025pairwise}).\nSix experimental controls (\\S\\ref{sec:amplification}) bound the amplification factor but cannot eliminate it; the attenuation evidence ($d \\to 0$ at $n = 355$) is necessary but not sufficient for causal identification.\nNon-tie concordance analysis partially adjudicates: among 201 Likert non-tie pairs (pooled across judges and axes), 76.6\\% agree with pairwise direction [70.3\\%, 81.9\\%], $p < 0.001$; point-biserial correlations are uniformly positive ($r = 0.20$--$0.45$, 7/9 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:concordance}), evidence more consistent with measurement attenuation than construct divergence, though partial construct divergence remains at weaker effect sizes.\nClaude's benchmark-dependent profile illustrates why aggregate BT is insufficient for bias attribution: LLMBar formality shows near-null sensitivity (Style OR n.s.) while AEval verbosity reveals significant sensitivity (Style OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$), a pattern disambiguated only by per-axis GEE decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_tost}).\nAll three interpretations converge on one practical conclusion: single-format audits risk incomplete bias coverage; the Blindspot manifests in 10/14 bias-positive cells, with four exceptions ($\\ddagger$) confirming effect-size dependence.\nFor alignment pipelines where pairwise judgments serve as reward signals, this implies that biases embedded during RLHF training may go undetected by Likert-based evaluation at standard benchmark scales.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/power_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection probability at observed effect magnitudes. 80\\% power crossings: sign test $n \\approx 62$, BT $n \\approx 114$, binarized Likert $n \\approx 462$, Likert ATE $n \\approx 1{,}473$. At typical scales ($n = 50$--$200$, shaded), pairwise methods reach 80\\% power while Likert methods remain far below. At $n = 355$, Likert $d = {-}0.025$ confirms the gap is structural, not merely a power issue.}\n\\label{fig:power_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Axis Confound Structure}\n\\label{sec:direction}\n\nGEE conditional decomposition \\citep{liang1986gee} with exchangeable working correlation and robust standard errors separates style from originality effects (Appendix~\\ref{app:direction}; model diagnostics including correlation structure robustness and interaction tests in Appendix~\\ref{app:gee_diagnostics}).\nAfter controlling for original preference, the formality effect remains significant (GPT-4o: OR $= 2.58$, $p = 0.002$; Qwen3-32B: OR $= 2.15$, $p < 0.0001$, $n = 355$; Llama-70B: OR $= 4.81$, $p < 0.0001$; Table~\\ref{tab:gee}); the significant originality ORs confirm residual confounding.\nFor formality, both style preference and quality correlation contribute (a conditional effect); the two-predictor GEE provides a parsimonious decomposition rather than causal identification; omitted variables (text length, lexical complexity) may partly confound both terms.\nThis per-axis heterogeneity has direct implications for bias mitigation: verbosity bias can be addressed by style-level interventions (e.g., length normalization or debiased prompting), whereas formality bias requires disentangling genuine quality correlates from surface-level preferences, a fundamentally harder problem that aggregate bias metrics cannot even detect.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\caption{GEE conditional decomposition (exchangeable correlation). Style OR: style-direction odds ratio; Orig OR: original-preference odds ratio (OR $> 1$ favors original). $n{=}88$ ORs are upward-biased estimates; scaled replications ($n = 330$--$355$) show $49$--$72\\%$ shrinkage \\citep{nemes2009bias}. Verbosity is style-dominated; formality shows varying confound structure.}\n\\label{tab:gee}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llclc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Style OR} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Originality OR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nConfig & OR & $p$ & OR & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 verb.\\ & 13.10 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.49 & .096 \\\\\nGPT-4o verb.\\ & 11.34 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.45 & .062 \\\\\nLlama-70B verb.\\ & 8.21 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.38 & .020 \\\\\nGemini verb.\\ & 8.98 & ${<}.0001$ & 0.46 & .061 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o form.\\ & 2.58 & .002 & 2.32 & .006 \\\\\nQwen3 form.\\ & 4.20 & .0002 & 4.70 & .0001 \\\\\nQwen3 form.$^a$ & 2.15 & ${<}.0001$ & 4.99 & ${<}.0001$ \\\\\nLlama-70B form.\\ & 4.81 & ${<}.0001$ & 3.12 & .0007 \\\\\nGemini form.\\ & 1.64 & .101 & 2.51 & .002 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o reg.\\ & 1.88 & .029 & 2.21 & .006 \\\\\nQwen3 reg.\\ & 1.91 & .046 & 5.72 & ${<}.0001$ \\\\\nLlama-70B reg.\\ & 3.33 & .0003 & 3.07 & .0008 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\footnotesize $^a$ $n = 355$; form.\\ $n{=}88$, verb.\\ $n{=}86$. 95\\% CIs in Appendix.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe confound structure is consistent across all four target judges for verbosity (style-dominated at $n = 86$--$88$: Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; confirmed at $n = 330$: Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$); formality is conditionally style-driven in three judges but originality-dominated in Gemini; register is judge-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:gee_decomp}).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm a rewriting artifact (Appendix~\\ref{app:artifact_control}), absorbed by GEE's originality term.\nThe Gemini exception (formality Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.) demonstrates that the decomposition is not uniformly biased toward detecting style effects; judges with weaker style sensitivity produce correspondingly weaker Style ORs.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/gee_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{GEE conditional decomposition across four judges ($n = 88$, LLMBar). Verbosity (left) is style-dominated: Style OR $\\gg 1$, Originality OR $< 1$. Formality (right) shows joint contributions from both predictors. Dashed line: OR $= 1$ (no effect). Hatched bars: Originality OR. Log scale.}\n\\label{fig:gee_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Sample-size sensitivity.}\nAt $n = 355$, the Qwen3-32B formality Style OR drops from $4.20$ to $2.15$ ($-49\\%$), consistent with small-sample upward bias \\citep{nemes2009bias}; the regime classification remains stable (both effects significant; Orig OR $4.70 \\to 4.99$).\nVerbosity $n = 330$ confirms 51--72\\% GEE shrinkage (Style OR $2.5$--$4.0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:verb_n355}), preserving the style-dominated classification; Likert begins detecting the strongest effects at this $n$, reframing the Blindspot as a $3$--$4\\times$ efficiency gap rather than absolute failure.\nThis convergence at larger $n$ is practically important: it means that labs with sufficient evaluation budgets ($n > 300$) can detect the strongest biases with Likert alone, but typical benchmark sizes ($n = 50$--$200$) systematically miss them.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Judge and Cross-Axis Replication}\n\\label{sec:cross_judge}\n\nTwo independent judges replicate the formality Blindspot: Qwen3-32B (BT 66.5\\%, $p < 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.461$) and Llama-3.3-70B (BT 69.5\\%, $p = 0.0001$; Likert $p = 0.534$).\nGemini-2.5-Flash shows a weaker, originality-dominated formality pattern (confirmatory: BT 57.4\\%, $p = 0.031$; Likert $d = {-}0.08$, n.s.; Style OR $= 1.64$, n.s.; Orig OR $= 2.51$, $p = 0.002$; \\S\\ref{sec:direction}).\nClaude Sonnet~4 shows near-null sensitivity on LLMBar for both axes (BT $\\leq 0.562$, n.s.), providing discriminative validity: CSD does not uniformly inflate bias estimates across all judges.\n\nVerbosity provides the strongest Blindspot: all four target judges show consistent preference (GPT-4o 76.0\\%, Qwen3-32B 77.3\\%, Llama-70B 73.5\\%, Gemini 70.8\\%; all $p \\leq 0.0001$), while Likert ATE remains non-significant for all ($p = 0.284$--$0.403$).\nGEE confirms style dominance (Style OR $8.2$--$13.1$, all $p < .0001$; Orig OR $0.38$--$0.49$; Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\nBoth rewriting directions favor verbose responses (90.7\\%/80.6\\%); length-controlled Style OR is unchanged ($<$2\\%), and an independent GPT-4o rewriter bounds evaluator-producer overlap to a 22\\% reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:rewriter_indep}).\nLength-controlled regression confirms 60\\% of the Flesch-Kincaid (FK) cross-axis effect is mediated by sentence length (Sobel $z = 2.15$, $p = .031$); under the assumed causal structure (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag}), FK acts as a plausible mediator ($S \\to \\text{FK} \\to Y$; \\citealp{pearl2009causality}), yielding a direct-effect Style OR of $2.15$ for GPT-4o (total OR $= 2.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:fk_length}).\n\nThese per-axis patterns selectively replicate across benchmarks (6/24 possible cells; Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}), targeting the strongest effects: Llama-70B formality on AlpacaEval (BT 75.0\\%, Style OR $= 7.64$) is strong enough for Likert detection ($\\ddagger$ rows), confirming the severity spectrum.\nThe selective replication strategy prioritizes cells where effects are strongest, providing an informative lower bound on cross-benchmark generalization without requiring exhaustive evaluation of all judge--axis--benchmark combinations.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/cross_benchmark_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Pairwise BT win probabilities across five judges, three benchmarks, and three style axes. Bold: $p < .05$; color intensity encodes significance level. Grey $\\times$: not tested. Claude cross-benchmark effects are quality-driven (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_judge}; Appendix~\\ref{app:claude_crossbench}). Full results in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench}.}\n\\label{fig:cross_bench_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nli_validation}\n\n\\paragraph{Content preservation.}\nTwo independent annotators evaluated 100 NLI-scored pairs (50 per axis; 70 gate-passed, 30 gate-failed), confirming $98.6\\%$ gate pass precision ($69/70$; Gwet's AC$_1 = 0.96$; Appendix~\\ref{app:annotation_agreement}).\n\\label{sec:nli_threshold}\nBT remains stable (${\\leq}3$pp variation) across 11 NLI thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_sweep}), confirming that results are not artifacts of a particular gating stringency.\nAnnotators judged only 49--66\\% of NLI-passed pairs as showing detectable style change, suggesting observed effects represent a diluted lower bound on true style sensitivity: the NLI gate retains pairs with subtle rewrites that human annotators cannot detect, yet judges still systematically prefer one variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection vs.\\ amplification.}\n\\label{sec:amplification}\n\\citet{jeong2025comparative} show that pairwise comparison amplifies style biases (the Comparative Trap).\nThe divergence cannot be definitively resolved without human ground-truth measurements; six controls bound amplification risk: (i)~NLI ablation reverses NLI-fail pairs (BT 41.7\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:nli_ablation}); (ii)~Claude shows benchmark-dependent sensitivity (formality Style OR n.s.; AEval verbosity OR $= 4.76$, $p < .001$); (iii)~cross-axis BT variation inconsistent with uniform amplification; (iv)~rewriting artifact absorbed by GEE; (v)~debiased prompting ($-5.2$/$-13.1$pp), bounding the amplification factor at ${\\sim}9$--$17\\%$ of the total BT effect; (vi)~four-architecture replication with consistent GEE structure.\nRegister is exploratory with evaluator-producer overlap and judge-dependent effects (Table~\\ref{tab:gee}).\nSame-style double-rewrite controls confirm that any residual rewriting artifact is absorbed by the GEE originality term rather than inflating the style coefficient.\nThe combination of NLI validation and amplification controls establishes that while the exact magnitude of style sensitivity remains bounded rather than point-identified, the qualitative finding (systematic style preferences invisible to Likert) is robust across judges, axes, and measurement assumptions.\n",
